Justification of the Benjamin-Ono equation as an internal water waves model
Martin Oen Paulsen

Abstract
. In this paper, we give the first rigorous justification of the Benjamin-Ono equation: ∂ t ζ + ( 1 − γ 2 ​ μ ​ | D | ) ​ ∂ x ζ + 3 ​ ε 2 ​ ζ ​ ∂ x ζ = 0 , (BO) \hskip 85.35826pt\partial_{t}\zeta+(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta\partial_{x}\zeta=0,\hskip 85.35826pt\text{(BO)} as an internal water wave model on the physical time scale. Here, ε {\varepsilon} is a small parameter measuring the weak nonlinearity of the waves, μ \mu is the shallowness parameter, and γ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \gamma\in(0,1) is the ratio between the densities of the two fluids. To be precise, we first prove the existence of a solution to the internal water wave equations for a two-layer fluid with surface tension, where one layer is of shallow depth and the other is of infinite depth. The existence time is of order 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ε ) \mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}) for a small amount of surface tension such that ε 2 ≤ bo − 1 {\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\mathrm{bo}^{-1} where bo \mathrm{bo} is the Bond number. Then, we show that these solutions are close, on the same time scale, to the solutions of the BO equation with a precision of order 𝒪 ⁡ ( μ + bo − 1 ) \mathcal{O}(\mu+\mathrm{bo}^{-1}) . In addition, we provide the justification of new equations with improved dispersive properties, the Benjamin equation, and the Intermediate Long Wave (ILW) equation in the deep-water limit. The long-time well-posedness of the two-layer fluid problem was first studied by Lannes [Arch. Ration. Mech. Anal., 208(2):481-567, 2013] in the case where both fluids have finite depth. Here, we adapt this work to the case where one of the fluid domains is of finite depth, and the other one is of infinite depth. The novelties of the proof are related to the geometry of the problem, where the difference in domains alters the functional setting for the Dirichlet-Neumann operators involved. In particular, we study the various compositions of these operators that require a refined symbolic analysis of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator on infinite depth and derive new pseudo-differential estimates that might be of independent interest.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 35Q35; Secondary: 76B55, 76B45

1.
Introduction

1.1.
The Benjamin-Ono equation

The Benjamin-Ono (BO) equation is a nonlocal asymptotic model for the unidirectional propagation of weakly nonlinear, long internal waves in a two-layer fluid. The equation is given by

(1.1)

\partial_{t}\zeta+(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta\partial_{x}\zeta=0,

where
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
t>0
and
\zeta=\zeta(x,t)
denote the free surface, which is a real-valued function. Here,
{\varepsilon}
is a small parameter measuring the weak nonlinearity of the waves,
\mu
is the shallowness parameter, and
\gamma\in(0,1)
is the ratio between the densities of the two fluids. The operator
|\mathrm{D}|
is a Fourier multiplier defined by

|\mathrm{D}|f(x)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big(|\xi|\mathcal{F}(f)(\xi)\big)(x).

The BO equation was introduced formally by Benjamin
[
14
]
in 1967 and at the same time independently by Davis and Acrivos
[
30
]
. We also refer the reader to the book by Klein and Saut
[
58
]
, Chapter 3, for a detailed state-of-the-art. The studies in
[
14
,
30
]
showed that the BO equation admits solitary waves with mere algebraic decay, as opposed to the exponential decay exhibited for the solitary waves of the KdV equation. Davis and Acrivos also gave experimental results. The experiments were carried out in a wave tank with a stratified solution of salt and water, where almost any disturbance to the surface
would, after a short time, produce a wave with a fixed shape that propagates stably. It was later noted by Ono
[
83
]
that the ease with which they could generate solitary waves indicates that they are solitons.

The paper by Ono sparked much interest in studying the dynamics of the BO equation. It was proved that the solitary waves are unique (up to translation)
[
12
]
, and the stability of these objects is studied in
[
17
,
92
,
55
]
(see the references for a precise definition). Moreover, the stability of these waves is strong enough to preserve its own identity upon nonlinear interactions. The strong interaction between several solitary waves is studied in
[
72
,
81
]
and relies on explicit formulas (see also
[
55
]
for the asymptotic stability of one soliton and
N-
solitons).

The fact that explicit solutions like the soliton (or multi-solitons) exist is a consequence of the complete integrability of the BO equation. Nakamura
[
80
]
proved the existence of an infinite number of conserved quantities and discovered a Lax pair structure (see also
[
18
,
35
,
41
]
). This insight is proven to be crucial for the study of the dynamics of the BO equation and was further developed by Gérard and Kapeller
[
41
]
. They constructed a nonlinear Fourier transform for the BO equation on the torus, which has several applications to low regularity well-posedness of the initial value problem, the long-time behavior of solutions, and stability of traveling waves (see
[
38
]
for a survey on this topic). More recently, Gérard
[
39
]
derived an explicit formula for the BO equation based on the Lax pair structure with remarkable consequences, for instance, the zero-dispersion limit problem
[
40
]
(see also
[
37
,
36
]
).

The Cauchy problem for BO has been extensively studied in the last 40 years. It was first proved to be globally well-posed in
H^{s}({\mathbb{R}})
for
s>\frac{3}{2}
using an energy method, see
[
1
,
49
]
. We also refer to the results
[
85
,
59
,
54
]
for an improvement by including the dispersive smoothing effects in the energy estimates.
One of the main difficulties in improving the result further is that the flow map fails to be
C^{2}
in any Sobolev space
H^{s}({\mathbb{R}})

[
78
]
(see also
[
60
]
). A breakthrough was achieved by Tao
[
90
]
, where he introduced a clever change of variables (the gauge transform) to improve the structure of the nonlinearity. As a consequence, he obtained a global well-posedness result for data in
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
. Several papers expanded on these ideas. We refer the interested reader to
[
21
,
48
,
76
,
45
]
for results on the line and
[
74
,
75
,
76
]
in the periodic case culminating in the global well-posedness in
L^{2}
. So far, the theory is based on PDE methods. However, by actively using the integrable structure, Gérard, Kappeler, and Topalov
[
42
]
proved the sharp global well-posedness result in
H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
for
s>-\frac{1}{2}
on the torus. Also, still relying on the integrability, Killip, Laurens, and Vişan
[
56
]
recently proved the global well-posedness in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
for
s>-\frac{1}{2}
on the line.

Figure 1.
The blue line denotes the surface elevation
z={\varepsilon}\zeta
and separates two fluids with density
0<\rho^{-}<\rho^{+}
.

1.1.1.
Full justification

Despite the rich well-posedness theory for the BO equation, it is still an open question whether its solutions are close to the ones of the original physical system. In the rigorous derivation of any asymptotic model, it is fundamental to know whether its solutions converge to the solutions of the reference model from which it is derived. The BO equation’s reference model is a coupled system of Euler equations for two fluids that are joined with an interface, as in Figure
1
. Under the irrotationality condition, we will call the reference model the “internal water waves system”. To prove that BO is a valid approximation, we shall compare their solutions to the physical parameters:

{\varepsilon}=\frac{a}{H}\quad,\quad\mu=\frac{H^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{bo}=\frac{\rho^{+}g\lambda^{2}(1-\gamma)}{\sigma},

where
a
is the typical amplitude of the waves,
H
is the still water depth in the lower fluid,
\lambda
is the typical wavelength,
\rho^{+}
is the density of the lower fluid,
g
is the acceleration of gravity,
\sigma
is the surface tension parameter. Since surface tension is only relevant for short waves, we will suppose that
\mathrm{bo}^{-1}
is small. To be precise, we will let
{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\mathrm{bo}^{-1}
, where we answer the following three questions:

(1)

The solutions of the internal water wave equations exist on the relevant time scale
\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
.

(2)

The solutions to the BO equation are uniformly controlled on the same time scale.

(3)

The
consistency
between the BO equation and the internal water wave equations, i.e., solutions of the internal water wave equations solves the BO equation up to a small reminder. Then use it to show that the error is of order
\mathcal{O}((\mu+\mathrm{bo}^{-1})t)
when comparing the two solutions.

The first point is the most challenging step of this paper, where we need to prove that the internal water waves equations are long-time well-posed for regular initial data. Moreover, we are confined to the specific geometry where one fluid layer is of shallow depth and the other of infinite depth. The main obstacle to constructing such solutions is the tendency of internal waves to break down due to Kelvin-Helmholtz instabilities. This issue was resolved in the case of a single fluid (i.e.,
\rho^{-}=0
), where stable solutions are deduced by imposing the
Rayleigh-Taylor criterion
:

(1.2)

-\partial_{z}P^{+}|_{z={\varepsilon}\zeta}>0,

where
z
is the vertical coordinate, and
P^{+}|_{z={\varepsilon}\zeta}
is the pressure at the surface. The physical relevance of this criterion can be seen by considering the Euler equations for a trivial flow
-\partial_{z}P^{+}|_{z={\varepsilon}\zeta}=\rho^{+}g
, where gravity
g>0
is the restoring force
. In this sense, the criterion is a natural condition to ensure that the pressure force is restoring and does not amplify the waves
[
91
]
. From a mathematical perspective, the criterion ensures the hyperbolicity of the water waves equation. Moreover, it is proved that under this condition, the water waves system in finite depth is locally well-posed by Lannes
[
63
]
and then long-time well-posed by Alvarez-Samaniego and Lannes
[
9
]
. We also refer the reader to the pioneering work of Wu
[
93
,
94
]
in the case of infinite depth where one of the key observations was the use of the Rayleigh-Taylor criterion (
1.2
) to remove a smallness condition on the data (see also the more recent work on extended life span and improved regularity results
[
95
,
96
,
97
,
7
,
5
,
6
,
3
,
2
]
).

In the case of two fluid systems, the internal water waves system produces Kelvin-Helmholtz instabilities and becomes ill-posed unless there is surface tension
\sigma>0

[
32
,
47
,
51
]
. There are several results on the well-posedness of the internal water wave systems in different configurations of the fluid domain where the time of existence depends on
\sigma

[
10
,
11
,
25
,
88
,
89
]
. On the other hand, the strength of surface tension is only relevant for very small values in water waves theory. Therefore, we must add surface tension to exploit its regularizing effect, but obtain a uniform local well-posedness result with respect to
\sigma>0
, allowing it to be taken small. Lannes solved this problem in
[
65
]
for two fluid layers of finite depth, where he derived a new stability criterion depending on
\sigma
. A crucial point is that surface tension could be taken small enough in the criterion such that it does not affect the main dynamics of the equation. It is also noted in the paper that the criterion depends strongly on the geometry of the problem. Many of the technical difficulties in this paper are related to this observation. In this work, we consider one of the layers to be of infinite depth. This is the key point of the paper, which will require a symbolic analysis of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator in a new functional setting. To achieve this goal, we derive several pseudo-differential estimates for symbols with limited smoothness.

The second point is well-known since the BO equation is globally well-posed for regular data (see the discussion above). However, we will consider three intermediate models to derive the BO equation. We will first derive a new weakly dispersive Benjamin-type system from the internal water waves system that is consistent with a precision of order
\mathcal{O}({\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu})
. The novelty of this system is that it is exact at the linear level and models exactly the dispersion relation of the internal water waves systems when
{\varepsilon}=0
. These types of systems are commonly referred to as fully dispersive, and the analysis is motivated by the work of Emerald
[
33
]
(see also
[
31
]
), where he derived fully dispersive shallow water models. Then, we will consider unidirectional solutions of this system to deduce a weakly dispersive Benjamin equation, which is exact at the linear level, and from it, we give the first rigorous derivation of the Benjamin equation
[
15
,
16
]
:

(1.3)

\partial_{t}\zeta+\big(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|-\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}}{2\mathrm{bo}}\big)\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta\partial_{x}\zeta=0.

We refer the reader to
[
57
]
for a detailed survey on the Benjamin equation and
[
70
,
24
,
61
,
69
]
for global well-posedness results. Then from (
1.3
) we may use it to derive the BO equation by neglecting the effect of surface tension. Finally, we also use this result to derive the ILW equation rigorously. The ILW was derived formally in
[
50
,
62
]
as an interface model where one of the fluid layers is of great depth, and its rigorous derivation remains an open problem. However, in the deep-water limit, it is well-known that its solutions converge to the ones of BO
[
23
,
68
]
(see also
[
44
,
8
,
79
,
77
,
22
]
for other results on the Cauchy problem). In this case, we can prove that the ILW approximates the internal water waves equations when one layer has infinite depth. To rigorously derive these models, we closely follow the work of Bona, Lannes, and Saut in
[
19
]
. In this paper, they derive several shallow water models for internal fluids and comment on the formal derivation of the BO equation.

Finally, we comment on several works that are closely related to the derivation of the BO equation. In
[
27
]
, Craig, Guyenne, and Kalisch used a Hamiltonian perturbation approach to formally derive asymptotic models from the two-layer system. Among the models is the BO equation. The benefit of this approach is that the systems inherit the Hamiltonian structure, but as noted in
[
58
]
, the process could lead to ill-posed systems. In particular, the BO system they derive, which can be used to derive the BO equation, is linearly ill-posed. In
[
43
]
, Ifrim, Rowan, Tataru, and Wan show that the BO equation can also be viewed as an asymptotic model from the water waves equations in infinite depth in the case of constant vorticity. The approximation they obtain is rigorously justified but, of course, not related to the asymptotic description of internal waves. Lastly, in
[
82
]
, Ohi and Iguchi proved the well-posedness of the internal water waves for one fluid of infinite depth to derive the BO equation. However, in their paper, the existence time is of order
\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{bo}^{-\frac{1}{2}})
, which is too short to justify the BO equation on the physical time scale. The technique is based on the one of Wu
[
93
]
, where the reference model is given in holomorphic coordinates. We will instead use a version of the Zakharov-Craig-Sulem formulation and closely follow the work of Lannes
[
65
]
.
In particular, the goal of this paper is to prove the long-time existence of the internal water waves equations with one fluid of infinite depth and positive surface tension. Then we show that the difference between two regular solutions of the internal water waves equations and the BO equation, which evolves from the same initial datum, is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\mu+\mathrm{bo}^{-1})
for all
0\leq t\lesssim{\varepsilon}^{-1}
for any
{\varepsilon},\mu\in(0,1)
such that
{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\mathrm{bo}^{-1}
.

1.2.
The governing equations

The basis of this study is the Euler equations for an irrotational two-layer fluid written in the Zakharov-Craig-Sulem formulation
[
99
,
28
,
29
]
. For the upper layer, we consider the following set of equations

(1.4)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta-\mathcal{G}^{-}[\zeta]\psi^{-}=0\\
\rho^{-}\Big(\partial_{t}\psi^{-}+g\zeta+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\psi^{-})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\mathcal{G}^{-}[\zeta]\psi^{-}+\partial_{x}\zeta\partial_{x}\psi^{-})^{2}}{1+(\partial_{x}\zeta)^{2}}\Big)=-P^{-}|_{z=\zeta}.\end{cases}

Here, the free surface elevation is the graph of
\zeta(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the function
P^{-}|_{z=\epsilon\zeta}
is the pressure force at the free surface. The function
\psi^{-}(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}
is the trace at the surface of the velocity potential solving the elliptic problem

(1.5)

\begin{cases}(\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{z}^{2})\Phi^{-}=0\quad\text{for}\quad\Omega^{-}=\{(x,z)\>:\>z>\zeta\}\\
\Phi^{-}|_{z=\zeta}=\psi^{-},\end{cases}

and
\mathcal{G}^{-}
is the negative Dirichlet-Neumann operator defined by

\mathcal{G}^{-}[\zeta]\psi^{-}=(\partial_{z}\phi^{-}-\partial_{x}\zeta\partial_{x}\phi^{-})|_{z=\zeta}.

For the fluid in the lower layer, the governing equations are given in terms of
(\zeta,\psi^{+})
and read

(1.6)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta-\mathcal{G}^{+}[\zeta]\psi^{+}=0\\
\rho^{+}\Big(\partial_{t}\psi^{+}+g\zeta+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\psi^{+})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\mathcal{G}^{+}[\zeta]\psi^{+}+\partial_{x}\zeta\partial_{x}\psi^{+})^{2}}{1+(\partial_{x}\zeta)^{2}}\Big)=-P^{+}|_{z=\zeta},\end{cases}

where the elliptic problem in the lower fluid is given by

(1.7)

\begin{cases}(\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{z}^{2})\Phi^{+}=0\quad\text{for}\quad\Omega^{+}=\{(x,z)\>:\>-H<z<\zeta\}\\
\Phi^{+}|_{z=\zeta}=\psi^{+}\quad\partial_{z}\Phi^{+}|_{z=-H}=0,\end{cases}

and the positive Dirichlet-Neumann operator is defined by

\mathcal{G}^{+}[\zeta]\psi^{+}=(\partial_{z}\phi^{+}-\partial_{x}\zeta\partial_{x}\phi^{+})|_{z=\zeta}.

To ease the notation, we make the following simplifications

\gamma=\frac{\rho^{-}}{\rho^{+}},\quad\rho^{-}<\rho^{+}=1,\quad g=1.

Moreover, we recall that the difference in pressure at the interface is proportional to the mean curvature of the interface:

P^{+}|_{z=\zeta}-P^{-}|_{z=\zeta}=\sigma\kappa(\zeta),

where
\sigma\in(0,1)
is the surface tension parameter and
\kappa(\zeta)
is defined by

(1.8)

\kappa(\zeta)=-\partial_{x}\Big(\frac{\partial_{x}\zeta}{\sqrt{1+(\partial_{x}\zeta)^{2}}}\Big).

We will now collect all these equations into one system, where we reduce the number of unknowns by using the first equation in (
1.6
) and (
1.4
) to see that

(1.9)

\mathcal{G}^{-}[\zeta]\psi^{-}=\mathcal{G}^{+}[\zeta]\psi^{+}.

In particular, we will prove later that we can write
\psi^{-}
as a function of
\psi^{+}
through the inverse relation

(1.10)

\psi^{-}=(\mathcal{G}^{-}[\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}[\zeta]\psi^{+}.

Then, we can define the new variable
\psi
by the formula

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle=\psi^{+}-\gamma\psi^{-}

\displaystyle=\big(1-\gamma(\mathcal{G}^{-}[\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}[\zeta]\big)\psi^{+}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}[\zeta]\psi^{+}.

The unknowns
\zeta
and
\psi
are the primary variables. We follow the work of Lannes
[
65
]
to show that we can use them to recover the velocity potentials
\Phi^{\pm}
through the transmission problem:

(1.11)

\begin{cases}\Delta_{x,z}\Phi^{\pm}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\qquad\text{in}\quad\Omega^{\pm}\\
\Phi^{+}|_{z=\zeta}-\gamma\Phi^{-}|_{z=\zeta}=\psi\\
\partial_{n}\Phi^{-}|_{{z=\zeta}}=\partial_{n}\Phi^{+}|_{{z=\zeta}},\quad\partial_{z}\Phi^{+}|_{z=-H}=0,\end{cases}

with
\psi^{\pm}=\Phi^{\pm}|_{z=\zeta}
and the normal condition on
z=\zeta
is the same as (
1.9
) where
\partial_{n}
stands for the upwards normal derivative. From these relations, it will be possible to reduce the two-fluid equations into a set of equations defined by
\zeta
and
\psi
where we formally define a new Dirichlet-Neumann operator that links the two fluids through the relation,

(1.12)

\mathcal{G}[\zeta]=\mathcal{G}^{+}[\zeta](\mathcal{J}[\zeta])^{-1}.

From the above expressions, we have the main governing equations (in dimensional form) that we will study throughout this paper:

(1.13)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta-\mathcal{G}[\zeta]\psi=0\\
\partial_{t}\psi+(1-\gamma)\zeta+\frac{1}{2}\big((\partial_{x}\psi^{+})^{2}-\gamma(\partial_{x}\psi^{-})^{2}\big)+\mathcal{N}[\zeta,\psi^{\pm}]=-\sigma\kappa(\zeta),\end{cases}

where

\mathcal{N}[\zeta,\psi^{\pm}]=\frac{\gamma(\mathcal{G}^{-}[\zeta]\psi^{-}+\partial_{x}\zeta\partial_{x}\psi^{-})^{2}-(\mathcal{G}^{+}[\zeta]\psi^{+}+\partial_{x}\zeta\partial_{x}\psi^{+})^{2}}{2(1+(\partial_{x}\zeta)^{2})}.

1.2.1.
Nondimensionalization of the equations

To derive an asymptotic model from (
1.13
), we will compare every variable and function with physical characteristic parameters of the same dimension
H,a
, or
\lambda
. Since the BO equation describes long waves, it is natural to consider the scaling:

x=\lambda x^{\prime},\quad\zeta=a\zeta^{\prime},

where the prime notation denotes a nondimensional quantity. To identify the remaining variables
\psi^{\prime}
and
t=(1-\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\lambda}{c_{\mathrm{ref}}}t^{\prime}
one needs information on the reference velocity
c_{\mathrm{ref}}
. To do so, we look at the linearized equations (with
\sigma=0
):

(1.14)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta-\mathcal{G}[0]\psi=0\\
\partial_{t}\psi+(1-\gamma)\zeta=0,\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{G}[0]
is a Fourier multiplier given by
1
1

1

See Remark
2.3
.

\mathcal{G}[0]\psi(x)=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\Big(|\xi|\frac{\mathrm{tanh}(H|\xi|)}{1+\gamma\mathrm{tanh}(H|\xi|)}\hat{\psi}(\xi)\Big)(x).

For a wave with typical wavelength
\lambda
, the frequencies are concentrated around
|\xi|=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda}
. Therefore, if we suppose that the depth of lower fluid is small compared to the wavelength, then we have by a Taylor expansion that

\mathcal{G}[0]\psi=-H\partial_{x}^{2}\psi,

up to higher order terms in
\mu
. From this simplification we can reduce (
1.14
) to a wave equation where we make the identification
c_{\mathrm{ref}}^{2}=H(1-\gamma)
, and from the second equation we find the dimensions of
\psi
:
2
2

2

The purpose of
(1-\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is to replace
(1-\gamma)\zeta
by
\zeta
in (
1.14
).

\psi=\frac{a\lambda(1-\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\sqrt{H}}\psi^{\prime}.

Lastly, we also choose to scale the transverse variable with
H
(i.e.,
z=Hz^{\prime}
) to have a reference domain in the lower fluid of unitary depth. Then applying these changes of variables and dropping the prime notation, we find that the nondimensional internal water waves system (
1.13
) is given by:

(1.15)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta-\frac{1}{\mu}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi=0\\
\partial_{t}\psi+\zeta+\frac{1}{2}\big({\varepsilon}(\partial_{x}\psi^{+})^{2}-\gamma{\varepsilon}(\partial_{x}\psi^{-})^{2}\big)+{\varepsilon}\mathcal{N}[{\varepsilon}\zeta,\psi^{\pm}]=-\frac{1}{\mathrm{bo}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}}\kappa({\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}\zeta),\end{cases}

where

\mathcal{N}[{\varepsilon}\zeta,\psi^{\pm}]=\frac{1}{2\mu}\frac{\gamma(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{-}+{\varepsilon}\mu\partial_{x}\zeta\partial_{x}\psi^{-})^{2}-(\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}+{\varepsilon}\mu\partial_{x}\zeta\partial_{x}\psi^{+})^{2}}{(1+\varepsilon^{2}\mu(\partial_{x}\zeta)^{2})}.

The operators
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{\pm}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]
are defined by

\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{\pm}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{\pm}=\sqrt{1+{\varepsilon}^{2}(\partial_{x}\zeta)^{2}}\partial_{n}\Phi^{\pm}|_{z={\varepsilon}\zeta},

through the solutions of the scaled Laplace equations:

\begin{cases}(\mu\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{z}^{2})\Phi^{+}=0\quad\text{for}\quad\Omega^{+}=\{(x,z)\>:\>-1<z<{\varepsilon}\zeta\}\\
\Phi^{+}|_{z={\varepsilon}\zeta}=\psi^{+}\quad\partial_{z}\Phi^{+}|_{z=-1}=0,\end{cases}

and

\begin{cases}(\mu\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{z}^{2})\Phi^{-}=0\quad\text{for}\quad\Omega^{-}=\{(x,z)\>:\>z>{\varepsilon}\zeta\}\\
\Phi^{-}|_{z={\varepsilon}\zeta}=\psi^{-}.\end{cases}

1.3.
Main results

In this paper we will first prove the well-posedness of (
1.15
) on a time scale
\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}})
for
{\varepsilon}\lesssim\sqrt{\mu}
and
\mathrm{bo}^{-1}={\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}
. To state this result, there are two fundamental assumptions that we need to make.

Definition 1.1

(Non-cavitation condition)
.

Let
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
,
s>\frac{1}{2}
and take
\zeta_{0}\in H^{s}({\mathbb{R}})
. We say
\zeta_{0}
satisfies the “non-cavitation condition”  if there exist
h_{\min}\in(0,1)
such that

(1.16)

h=1+{\varepsilon}\zeta_{0}(x)\geq h_{\min},\quad\text{for all}\>\>\>x\in\mathbb{R}.

The second condition is to ensure the solutions do not break down due to Kelvin-Helmholtz instabilities and is key to showing the long-time existence for solutions of (
1.15
). The criterion is enforced for data in the energy space, which we will now define.

Definition 1.2

(Energy space)
.

Let
{\varepsilon},\mu,\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\in(0,1)
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then we define the function space
H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})
by

H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})=H^{N+1}({\mathbb{R}}),

with norm

|u|_{H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}}^{2}=|u|_{H^{N}}^{2}+\mathrm{bo}^{-1}|\partial_{x}u|^{2}_{H^{N}}.

We define
\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
as a Beppo-Levi space

\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})=\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})=\{u\in L^{2}_{loc}({\mathbb{R}})\>:\>\partial_{x}u\in H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})\},

endowed with

|u|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}=\Big|\frac{|\mathrm{D}|}{(1+\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}u\Big|_{H^{s}}.

Moreover, let
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}
and define the “good unknowns”  by

\zeta_{(\alpha)}=\partial_{x,t}^{\alpha}\zeta,\quad\psi_{(\alpha)}=\partial_{x,t}^{\alpha}\psi-{\varepsilon}\underline{w}\partial_{x,t}^{\alpha}\zeta.

Then the natural energy space
\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{bo},T}^{N}
associated to (
1.15
) is defined for functions
\mathbf{U}=(\zeta_{(\alpha)},\psi_{(\alpha)})
in

(1.17)

\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{bo},T}^{N}=\{\mathbf{U}\in C([0,T];H^{N}({\mathbb{R}})\times\dot{H}^{t_{0}+3}({\mathbb{R}}),\quad\sup\limits_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{E}^{N,t_{0}}_{\mathrm{bo},\mu}(\mathbf{U}(t))<\infty\},

where

(1.18)

\mathcal{E}^{N,t_{0}}_{\mathrm{bo},\mu}(\mathbf{U})=|\partial_{x}\psi|_{H^{t_{0}+2}}^{2}+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2},|\alpha|\leq N}|\zeta_{(\alpha)}|_{H^{1}_{\mathrm{bo}}}^{2}+|\psi_{(\alpha)}|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}}.

Remark 1.3
.

Here, the energy depends on both time derivatives and spatial derivatives. This is the method put forward by
[
86
,
87
,
73
]
to control the surface tension term for the water wave equations (see Remark
5.1
for the specifics on this point). This method was later used for the internal water waves equations with surface tension in the case of two fluids of finite depth
[
65
]
, which is one of the primary references of this paper.

Definition 1.4

(Stability criterion)
.

Let
\mathbf{U}_{0}=(\zeta_{0},\psi_{0})\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\times\dot{H}_{\mu}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
and
\mathcal{E}^{N,t_{0}}_{\mathrm{bo},\mu}(\mathbf{U}_{0})<\infty
. Then we define the “stability criterion”  by

(1.19)

\qquad 0<\mathfrak{d}(\mathbf{U}):=\inf\limits_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathfrak{a}-\Upsilon\mathfrak{c}(\zeta)|[\![\underline{V}^{\pm}]\!]|_{L^{\infty}}^{4},\quad\text{at}\>\>\>t=0,

where

\Upsilon=\frac{\mathrm{bo}}{2}(1-\gamma)^{2}\gamma^{2}{\varepsilon}^{4}\mu,

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}

\displaystyle=\Big(1+{\varepsilon}\big((\partial_{t}+{\varepsilon}\underline{V}^{+}\partial_{x})\underline{w}^{+}-\gamma(\partial_{t}+{\varepsilon}\underline{V}^{-}\partial_{x})\underline{w}^{-}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{e}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{f\in H^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}}),f\neq 0}\mu\frac{\big((\mathcal{J}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\partial_{x}f,\partial_{x}f\big)_{L^{2}}}{|1+\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|^{\frac{1}{2}}f|_{L^{2}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{e}(\zeta)^{2}(1+{\varepsilon}^{2}\mu|\partial_{x}\zeta|_{L^{\infty}}^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}.

The quantities
\underline{V}^{\pm}
,
\underline{w}^{\pm}
describe the horizontal and vertical velocity field in the fluids and are given in Definition
4.1
. See also Corollary
A.18
in the Appendix, where they are given in terms of
\zeta
and
\psi
. While
\mathfrak{e}(\zeta)
is defined in (
4.17
) such that it is uniform with respect to
\mu
, see Proposition
4.4
.

Remark 1.5
.

The stability criterion (
1.19
) can be seen as a two-layer generalization of the Rayleigh-Taylor criterion where

\mathfrak{a}=-\big(\partial_{z}P^{+}-\gamma\partial_{z}P^{-}\big)|_{z={\varepsilon}\zeta}>\Upsilon\mathfrak{c}(\zeta)|[\![\underline{V}^{\pm}]\!]|_{L^{\infty}}^{4}.

We will let
{\varepsilon}^{2}\mathrm{bo}
to be of order one. In this case, the size of the quantity
\Upsilon
is of order
\mathcal{O}({\varepsilon}^{2}\mu)
and is neglected in the BO regime and the models considered in this paper.

Remark 1.6
.

One key difference with the work of Lannes
[
65
]
for the internal water waves on finite depth is in the symbolic analysis of
\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]
. The operator depends on the solution of an elliptic problem on a domain with infinite depth. This alters the functional setting, where we also need precise estimates depending on the parameters
{\varepsilon},\mu\in(0,1)
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
t_{0}=1
,
N\geq 5
,
{\varepsilon},\mu,\gamma,\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\in(0,1)
such that
{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\mathrm{bo}^{-1}
. Assume that
\mathbf{U}_{0}=(\zeta_{0},\psi_{0})^{T}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})\times\dot{H}_{\mu}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
such that
\mathcal{E}^{N,t_{0}}_{\mathrm{bo},\mu}(\mathbf{U}_{0})<\infty
. Suppose further that
\mathbf{U}_{0}
satisfies the non-cavitation condition (
1.16
) and the stability criterion (
1.19
). Then for nondecreasing function of its argument
C=C(\mathcal{E}^{N}(\mathbf{U}_{0}),{h^{-1}_{\min}},\mathfrak{d}(\mathbf{U}_{0})^{-1})>0
there exist a time

T=C^{-1},

and a unique solution
\mathbf{U}=(\zeta,\psi)^{T}\in\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{bo},{\varepsilon}^{-1}T}^{N}
of (
1.15
). Moreover, the solution satisfies

(1.20)

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]}\mathcal{E}^{N,t_{0}}_{\mathrm{bo},\mu}(\mathbf{U})\leq C\mathcal{E}^{N,t_{0}}_{\mathrm{bo},\mu}(\mathbf{U}_{0}).

Remark 1.8
.

For notational convenience we shall write
\mathcal{E}^{N}(\mathbf{U})
instead of
\mathcal{E}^{N,t_{0}}_{\mathrm{bo},\mu}(\mathbf{U})
. We also consider the case of one horizontal dimension since our primary goal is to justify the BO equation, which is a model that does not include transverse effects. We will deal with the higher dimensional case in a forthcoming paper.

Remark 1.9
.

The local well-posedness of (
1.15
) was first proved by Ohi and Iguchi
[
82
]
. However, in their paper, the existence time is of order
\mathcal{O}(\mathrm{bo}^{-\frac{1}{2}})
, which is far too short to justify the BO equation on the physical time scale. In fact, Theorem
1.7
is the first proof of the long-time well-posedness of the internal water waves in the case where one layer is of infinite depth.

Remark 1.10
.

The surface tension term is regularizing and plays a fundamental role in the well-posedness of (
1.15
). However, as noted in Remark
1.5
, it does not affect the dynamics of the BO equation.

Remark 1.11
.

It is also possible to let the top fluid layer be of finite depth and let the lower fluid layer be of infinite depth. The crucial point is to have a lower density in the top layer and the choice of the order of composition for Dirichlet-Neumann operators appearing in (
1.10
). In particular, if
\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]
corresponds to the operator for infinite depth, then one would need to simply change the order of composition in the proof (see Remark
2.5
).

Having defined a solution of the reference model (
1.15
) on a long time scale, the next step is to derive the asymptotic models. Here, we follow the road map in
[
19
]
, where they derived several internal water wave models in finite depth and gave comments on the formal derivation of the BO equation. In particular, it is convenient to write (
1.15
) in terms of
\psi^{+}\in\dot{H}^{\frac{3}{2}}_{\mu}({\mathbb{R}})
through the interface operator:

(1.21)

\mathbf{H}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}=\partial_{x}\psi^{-}\in H^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}}),

where
\psi^{-}=\Phi^{-}|_{z={\varepsilon}\zeta}\in\mathring{H}^{\frac{3}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
and
\Phi^{-}\in\dot{H}^{2}(\Omega^{-})
is the unique solution
3
3

3

See Proposition
2.4
.
(up to a constant) of

(1.22)

\begin{cases}(\mu\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{z}^{2})\Phi^{-}=0\hskip 95.3169pt\qquad\text{in}\quad\Omega^{-}\\
\partial_{n}\Phi^{-}=(1+{\varepsilon}^{2}(\partial_{x}\zeta)^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}\hskip 35.56593pt\text{on}\quad z={\varepsilon}\zeta.\end{cases}

Then we may define the the velocity variable

(1.23)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\psi

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\psi^{+}-\gamma\mathbf{H}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+},

and apply a derivative to the second equation of (
1.15
) to find that

(1.24)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta-\frac{1}{\mu}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}=0\\
\partial_{t}v+\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\partial_{x}\big((\partial_{x}\psi^{+})^{2}-\gamma(\mathbf{H}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+})^{2}\big)+{\varepsilon}\partial_{x}\mathcal{N}[{\varepsilon}\zeta,\psi^{\pm}]=-\frac{1}{\mathrm{bo}}\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}}\partial_{x}\kappa({\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}\zeta).\end{cases}

As noted in the introduction, we will first derive a system from (
1.24
), where we will show that a solution of the internal water waves equations, with regular data
(\zeta_{0},v_{0})
, solves a weakly dispersive Benjamin system. In particular, we define the Fourier multipliers

(1.25)

\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})=\frac{1}{1+\gamma\tanh(\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)}\frac{\tanh(\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)}{\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|},\quad\mathrm{k}(\mathrm{D})=\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\big(1-\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\partial_{x}^{2}\big),

and the function

u=\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})v.

Then we will show that the solution of the internal water waves equations satisfies

(1.26)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta+\partial_{x}u+{\varepsilon}\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}(\zeta u)=0\\
\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{k}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}(u)^{2}=0,\end{cases}

up to an error of order
\mathcal{O}({\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu})
, where give precise Sobolev bounds on the error in terms of the initial data. Then, under an additional assumption on the data (for right-moving waves), we will show that we can approximate this system with the solutions of a weakly dispersive version of the Benjamin equation

(1.27)

\partial_{t}\zeta+\sqrt{k}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta\partial_{x}\zeta=0,

up to an error of order
\mathcal{O}({\varepsilon}^{2}+{\varepsilon}\mathrm{bo}^{-1}+{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu})
. Then if we simplify the multiplier
\sqrt{k}(\mathrm{D})
we will show that one get the Benjamin equation

\partial_{t}\zeta+\big(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|-\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}}{2\mathrm{bo}}\big)\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta\partial_{x}\zeta=0,

up to an error
\mathcal{O}({\varepsilon}^{2}+{\varepsilon}\mathrm{bo}^{-1}+\sqrt{\mu}\mathrm{bo}^{-1}+\mu)
. Moreover, if we neglect the effect of surface tension we obtain the Benjamin-Ono equation.

Remark 1.12
.

Since we need to prove a consistency result to link the internal water waves equations with the BO equation, we also use it to derive some new models. These models have the added benefit of being fully dispersive; meaning they are exact at the linear level. Several extensions could be made, for instance, derivation of Green-Naghdi type models
[
26
]
, counterpropagating BO equations
[
13
]
, or improving the precision of (
1.27
) by following the analysis of
[
34
]
.

Remark 1.13
.

An alternative approach would be to rigorously derive the regularized BO system given by:

(1.28)

\begin{cases}\big(1+\alpha\sqrt{\mu}\gamma|\mathrm{D}|\big)\partial_{t}\zeta+\big(1+(\alpha-1)\gamma\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|\big)\partial_{x}v+{\varepsilon}\partial_{x}(\zeta v)=0\\
\partial_{t}v+\partial_{x}\zeta+{\varepsilon}v\partial_{x}v=0,\end{cases}

for
\alpha\geq 0
. This is the model that was formally derived in
[
19
]
, and moreover we can use it to derive the “regularized Benjamin-Ono equation”
[
29
]
:

\big(1+\alpha\sqrt{\mu}\gamma|\mathrm{D}|\big)\partial_{t}\zeta+\partial_{x}\zeta+(2\alpha-1)\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta\partial_{x}\zeta=0.

The rigorous derivation of these models is straightforward when having Theorem
1.7
and a consistency result, if we combine it with the long-time well-posedness of (
1.28
) provided by Xu in
[
98
]
for
\alpha>1
.

Before we proceed, we establish the long-time well-posedness of the weakly dispersive system introduced above. To do so, we must define the energy space associated with (
1.26
).

Definition 1.14
.

Let
s\geq 0
and
\mu,\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\in(0,1)
. Then we define the norm on the function space
X^{s}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})
to be

|(\zeta,u)|_{X^{s}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}}^{2}:=|\zeta|_{H^{s+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}}^{2}+|u|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\sqrt{\mu}||\mathrm{D}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u|_{H^{s}}^{2}.

Theorem 1.15
.

Let
{\varepsilon},\mu,\gamma,\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\in(0,1)
and
s>\frac{3}{2}
. Assume that
(\zeta_{0},u_{0})\in X^{s}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})
. Then there exists a
C=C(h_{\min}^{-1},|(\zeta_{0},u_{0})|_{X^{s}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}})>0
nodecreasing function of its argument and a time

T=C^{-1},

such that there exist a unique solution
(\zeta^{\text{\tiny wBs}},u^{\text{\tiny wBs}})\in C([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]:X^{s}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
to the weakly dispersive Benjamin system (
1.26
) that satisfies

(1.29)

\sup\limits_{t\in[0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]}|(\zeta^{\text{\tiny wBs}},u^{\text{\tiny wBs}})|_{X^{s}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}}\leq C|(\zeta_{0},u_{0})|_{X^{s}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}}.

Remark 1.16
.

System (
1.26
) and (
1.27
) are new and are chosen such that it is easy to deduce the long-time existence. The choice was based on the observations made in
[
84
]
, where weakly dispersive shallow water models are considered and can, in some cases, give rise to well-posed systems while their strongly dispersive versions are not.

Remark 1.17
.

For the data
\zeta_{0}\in H^{s}({\mathbb{R}})
with
s>\frac{3}{2}
the long-time (global) well-posedness of the Benjamin equation and the Benjamin-Ono equation is classical. The long time well-posedness can easily be extended for (
1.27
) with
{\varepsilon}\in(0,1)
. See, for instance,
[
71
]
in the case of the Benjamin-Ono equation on a fixed time with
{\varepsilon}=1
.

With this result in hand, we can use it as a link to prove the consistency between the BO equation and the internal water waves equations.

Theorem 1.18
.

Let
{\varepsilon},\mu,\gamma,\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\in(0,1)
, such that
{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\mathrm{bo}^{-1}
. Assume that
\mathbf{U}_{0}=(\zeta_{0},\psi_{0})^{T}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
1.7
and define
v_{0}=\partial_{x}\psi_{0}
. Then for
v=\partial_{x}\psi
there exists
C=C(\mathcal{E}^{N}(\mathbf{U}_{0}),{h^{-1}_{\min}},\mathfrak{d}(\mathbf{U}_{0})^{-1})>0
nondecreasing function of its argument and a time
T=C^{-1}
such that

(1.30)

\sup_{t\in[0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]}\big(|\zeta|_{H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}}^{2}+|v|_{H^{N-\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\big)\leq C.

Moreover, there exists a generic function
R
satisfying

|R|_{H^{N-5}}\leq C,

for all
t\in[0,\varepsilon^{-1}T]
such that we have the following results:

1.

There exists a unique solution
(\zeta,v)^{T}\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\times H^{N-\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
to (
1.24
), where
v=\partial_{x}\psi
. Moreover, on the same time interval, for

\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})=\frac{1}{1+\gamma\tanh(\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)}\frac{\tanh(\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)}{\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|},\quad\mathrm{k}(\mathrm{D})=\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\big(1-\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\partial_{x}^{2}\big),

and
u=\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})v
we have that the solution also satisfies

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta+\partial_{x}u+{\varepsilon}\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}(\zeta u)={\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}R\\
\partial_{t}u+\mathrm{k}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}(u)^{2}={\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}R.\end{cases}

2.

There exist a unique solution
\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
that the solves weakly dispersive Benjamin equation

\partial_{t}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}+\sqrt{k}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}=0.

Suppose further that the data
v_{0}
is given by

(1.31)

v_{0}=\mathrm{t}^{-1}(\mathrm{D})\big(\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}(\mathrm{D})\zeta_{0}^{\text{\tiny wB}}-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{4}(\zeta_{0}^{\text{\tiny wB}})^{2}\big),

and define
v^{\text{\tiny wB}},u^{\text{\tiny wB}}\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N-\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
by

u^{\text{\tiny wB}}=\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})v^{\text{\tiny wB}}=\big(\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}(\mathrm{D})\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{4}(\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}})^{2}\big).

Then the solution
(\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}},u^{\text{\tiny wB}})
also satisfies

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}+\partial_{x}u^{\text{\tiny wB}}+{\varepsilon}\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}(\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}u^{\text{\tiny wB}})=\varepsilon(\varepsilon+\sqrt{\mu}+\mathrm{bo}^{-1})R\\
\partial_{t}u^{\text{\tiny wB}}+\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{{x}}\big(u^{\text{\tiny wB}}\big)^{2}=\varepsilon(\varepsilon+\sqrt{\mu}+\mathrm{bo}^{-1})R.\end{cases}

3.

There exist a unique solution
\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
that solves the Benjamin equation

\partial_{t}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}+\big(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|-\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}}{2\mathrm{bo}}\big)\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}=0,

and on the same time interval it satisfies

\partial_{t}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}+\sqrt{k}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}=(\mu+\sqrt{\mu}\mathrm{bo}^{-1})R.

4.

There exist a unique solution
\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
that solves

\partial_{t}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}+\big(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|\big)\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}=0,

and on the same time interval it satisfies

\partial_{t}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}+\big(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|-\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}}{2\mathrm{bo}}\big)\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}=\mathrm{bo}^{-1}R.

Remark 1.19
.

The results in Theorem
1.18
are stated in the one-dimensional case, but the derivation of (
1.26
) can be extended to higher dimensions (see Remark
8.3
).

A consequence of the above results is the full justification of BO and related equations.

Theorem 1.20
.

Let
{\varepsilon},\mu,\gamma,\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\in(0,1)
, such that
{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\mathrm{bo}^{-1}
. Assume that
\mathbf{U}_{0}=(\zeta_{0},\psi_{0})^{T}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
1.7
with
N\geq 8
and define
v=\partial_{x}\psi
with
v_{0}=\partial_{x}\psi_{0}
. Suppose further that
v_{0}
satisfies (
1.31
). Then there exists
C=C(\mathcal{E}^{N}(\mathbf{U}_{0}),{h^{-1}_{\min}},\mathfrak{d}(\mathbf{U}_{0})^{-1})>0
nondecreasing function of its argument and a time
T=C^{-1}
such that:

1.

There exist a unique solution
(\zeta,v)\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\times H^{N-\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
to (
1.24
).

2.

From the same initial data:

2.1.

There exists a unique solution
(\zeta^{\text{\tiny wBs}},u^{\text{\tiny wBs}})\in C([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]:X^{N-\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
, where
u^{\text{\tiny wB}}=\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})v^{\text{\tiny wB}}
, to the weakly dispersive Benjamin system

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\zeta+\partial_{x}u^{\text{\tiny wBs}}+{\varepsilon}\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}(\zeta^{\text{\tiny wBs}}u^{\text{\tiny wBs}})=0\\
\partial_{t}u^{\text{\tiny wBs}}+\mathrm{k}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wBs}}+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\mathrm{t}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}(u^{\text{\tiny wBs}})^{2}=0,\end{cases}

and for any
t\in[0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]
there holds,

(1.32)

|(\zeta-\zeta^{\text{\tiny wBs}},v-v^{\text{\tiny wBs}})|_{L^{\infty}([0,t]:H^{N-7}\times H^{N-7})}\leq\varepsilon\sqrt{\mu}tC.

2.2.

There exists a unique solution
\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
to the weakly dispersive Benjamin equation

\partial_{t}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}+\sqrt{\mathrm{k}}(\mathrm{D})\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}=0

and for any
t\in[0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]
there holds,

(1.33)

|\zeta-\zeta^{\text{\tiny wB}}|_{L^{\infty}([0,t]:H^{N-7})}\leq\varepsilon({\varepsilon}+\sqrt{\mu}+\mathrm{bo}^{-1})tC.

2.3.

There exists a unique solution
\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
to the Benjamin equation

\partial_{t}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}+\big(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|-\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}}{2\mathrm{bo}}\big)\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}+\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}=0,

and for any
t\in[0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]
there holds,

(1.34)

|\zeta-\zeta^{\text{\tiny B}}|_{L^{\infty}([0,t]:H^{N-7})}\leq\big({\varepsilon}^{2}+{\varepsilon}\mathrm{bo}^{-1}+\sqrt{\mu}\mathrm{bo}^{-1}+\mu\big)tC.

2.4.

There exists a unique solution
\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
to the BO equation

\partial_{t}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}+c(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}+c\frac{3{\varepsilon}}{2}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}\partial_{x}\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}=0,

and for any
t\in[0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]
there holds,

(1.35)

|\zeta-\zeta^{\text{\tiny BO}}|_{L^{\infty}([0,t]:H^{N-7})}\leq\big(\mu+\ \mathrm{bo}^{-1})tC.

Remark 1.21
.

space

•

Estimates (
1.32
), (
1.33
), and (
1.34
) together with the well-posedness theory imply the full justification of their respective systems as internal water waves equations. These are new results, but their primary purpose is to serve as an intermediate step for the derivation of the BO equation and are added here for the sake of completeness.

•

Several asymptotic regimes are not captured by the result of this paper and the one of Lannes
[
65
]
. An interesting example would be the intermediate long wave regime where one layer is allowed to be larger than the other (see
[
58
,
19
,
57
]
). This will require a new well-posedness result for the internal water waves equations where one would have to track the dependencies in the depths carefully.

•

The Intermediate Long Wave equation (ILW) is a particular deep water model closely related to the BO equation. To be precise, the ILW equation is given by

(1.36)

\partial_{t}\zeta+(1-\frac{\gamma}{2}\sqrt{\mu}\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{D}))\partial_{x}\zeta+\frac{3\varepsilon}{2}\zeta\partial_{x}\zeta=0,

where
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{D})=|\mathrm{D}|\coth(\sqrt{\mu^{-}}|\mathrm{D}|)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{-}}}

and
\sqrt{\mu^{-}}=\frac{H^{-}}{\lambda}
, and
H^{-}
is the depth of upper fluid domain and
\lambda
is the typical wavelength. Then as
H^{-}\rightarrow\infty
, we observe that the symbol associated with
\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{D})
converges to the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}
, whose symbol in frequency reads
-i\text{sign}(\xi)
. In fact, it has been proved that the solutions of (
1.36
) converge to the ones of BO, even on
L^{2}(M)
for
M={\mathbb{R}}
or
\mathbb{T}

[
23
]
.

From Theorem
1.20
, one can prove as a Corollary, that the ILW equation provides a good approximation of the internal water waves equations if
(\frac{\mu}{\mu^{-}})^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mu+\mathrm{bo}^{-1}
is small.

Corollary 1.22
.

Let
N\geq 8
, and
{\varepsilon},\mu,\gamma,(\mu^{-})^{-1},\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\in(0,1)
, such that
{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\mathrm{bo}^{-1}
. Assume that
\mathbf{U}_{0}=(\zeta_{0},\psi_{0})^{T}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
1.20
. Then the solutions
\zeta,\zeta^{\text{\tiny ILW}}\in C\big([0,{\varepsilon}^{-1}T]\>:\>H^{N}({\mathbb{R}})\big)
of (
1.24
) and (
1.36
) satisfies the convergence estimate

(1.37)

|\zeta-\zeta^{\text{\tiny ILW}}|_{L^{\infty}([0,t]:H^{N-7})}\leq\big((\frac{\mu}{\mu^{-}})^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mu+\mathrm{bo}^{-1})tC.

1.3.1.
Strategy and outline of the proofs

The main body of the paper is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.7
, which relies on energy estimates similar to the ones provided by Lannes
[
65
]
. To do so, we first need to prove that the operators involved in the main system (
1.15
) are well-defined and can be formulated solely in terms of
\zeta
and
\psi
. We start by studying the operator
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}
in Section
2
, which will be given by the expression:

(1.38)

\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi=\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\Big(1-\gamma(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\Big)^{-1}\psi.

The main difference with the work of Lannes is that we have the composition of two operators
\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}
that act on different spaces. This is a consequence of having one fluid of finite depth and the other of infinite depth. In particular, to define the composition
(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{-})^{-1}
with
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{+}
we need to work on a homogeneous
4
4

4

The function space with
|f|_{\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}=||\mathrm{D}|^{\frac{1}{2}}f|_{H^{s}}
.
type target space.

The next step is to give a symbolic description of the operators involved in the expression of
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}
. This involves some of the key estimates that will be used to close the energy estimates. To do so, we need a symbolic description of each operator involved in (
1.38
). The key estimate, which is proved in Section
3
reads,

|\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{-}-\big(-\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|\psi^{-}\big)|_{H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\leq{\varepsilon}\mu C\big(|\zeta|_{H^{t_{0}+3}}\big)|\psi^{-}|_{\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}.

The symbolic approximation of
\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}
is well-known; see the collection of work by Lannes, Alazard, Metivier, Burq, and Zuily
[
63
,
7
,
5
,
6
]
for similar estimates. Their results are sharper in the sense that they require less regularity on
\zeta
. However, the symbolic description they provide is without the parameters
{\varepsilon},\mu
, and more importantly, is given on the space
\psi^{-}\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
. More precisely, the main difference is that we need to account for the small parameters and arrive at the space
\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
to close the estimates later. This result seems new and could be of independent interest. We refer the reader to Remark
3.3
for the novelties of the proof and possible extensions.

The last step before providing the a priori estimates is the quasilinearization of the internal water wave system. This is done in Section
4
, where we only give detailed proofs of the steps unique to the current setting. In fact, we can reduce some of the proofs to the estimates performed in
[
65
]
. We also provide details on the derivation of the stability criterion in Proposition
4.6
, where we obtain coercivity type estimates. From these results, we establish a priori bounds on the solution in Section
5
. Then, we use them to prove Theorem
1.7
in Section
6
.

For the remainder of the paper, we give the details on the derivation of the BO equation. We also provide a complete justification of the models that link the BO equation with the internal water waves equations. In Section
8
, we follow the work of Bona, Lannes, and Saut
[
19
]
to derive the intermediate systems provided in Theorem
1.18
and the BO equation. Then, in Section
7
, we give a short proof of Theorem
1.15
, which gives the long-time well-posedness of a weakly dispersive Benjamin system. Lastly, in Section
9
, we conclude the paper by proving Theorem
1.20
, i.e., the full justification of each of the systems derived in Theorem
1.18
, and then a short proof of Corollary
1.22
.

1.4.
Definition and notations

•

We define the gradient by

\nabla^{\mu}_{x,z}=(\sqrt{\mu}\partial_{x},\partial_{z})^{T}

and we introduce the scaled Laplace operator

\Delta^{\mu}_{x,z}=\nabla^{\mu}_{x,z}\cdot\nabla^{\mu}_{x,z}=\mu\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{z}^{2}.

•

We let
c
denote a positive constant independent of
\mu,{\varepsilon}
, and
\mathrm{bo}
that may change from line to line. Also, as a shorthand, we use the notation
a\lesssim b
to mean
a\leq c\>b
.

•

Let
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
be the usual space of square integrable functions with norm
|f|_{L^{2}}=\sqrt{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|f(x)|^{2}\>dx}
. Also, for any
f,g\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
we denote the scalar product by
\big(f,g\big)_{L^{2}}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}f(x)\overline{g(x)}\>dx
.

•

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a tempered distribution, let
\hat{f}
or
\mathcal{F}f
be its Fourier transform. Let
F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function. Then the Fourier multiplier associated with
F(\xi)
is denoted
\mathrm{F}
and defined by the formula:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\big(\mathrm{F}(\mathrm{D})f(x)\big)(\xi)=F(\xi)\hat{f}(\xi).

•

For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
we call the multiplier
\widehat{|\mathrm{D}|^{s}f}(\xi)=|\xi|^{s}\hat{f}(\xi)
the Riesz potential of order
-s
.

•

For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
we call the multiplier
\Lambda^{s}=(1+\mathrm{D}^{2})^{\frac{s}{2}}=\langle\mathrm{D}\rangle^{s}
the Bessel potential of order
-s
.

•

The Sobolev space
H^{s}(\mathbb{R})
is equivalent to the weighted
L^{2}-
space with
|f|_{H^{s}}=|\Lambda^{s}f|_{L^{2}}
.

•

For any
s\geq 0
we will denote
\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
a homogeneous type Sobolev space of degree
\frac{1}{2}
with
|f|_{\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}=||\mathrm{D}|^{\frac{1}{2}}f|_{H^{s}}
. One should note that
|\mathrm{D}|=\mathcal{H}\partial_{x}
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{H}f}(\xi)=-i\>\text{sgn}(\xi)\hat{f}(\xi)
is the Hilbert transform.

•

For any
s\geq 0
we will denote
\dot{H}^{s+1}(\mathbb{R})
the Beppo-Levi space with
|f|_{\dot{H}^{s+1}}=|\Lambda^{s}\partial_{x}f|_{L^{2}}
.

•

For any
s\geq 0
we will denote
\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})=\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
with
|f|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}=|\mathfrak{P}f|_{L^{2}}
and where
\mathfrak{P}
is a Fourier multiplier defined in frequency by:

\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{P}f)(\xi)=\frac{|\xi|}{(1+\sqrt{\mu}|\xi|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\hat{f}(\xi).

•

For any
s\geq 0
,
\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\in(0,1)
we denote
H^{s+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})=H^{s+1}({\mathbb{R}})

with norm

|u|_{H^{s+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}}^{2}=|u|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\mathrm{bo}^{-1}|\partial_{x}u|^{2}_{H^{s}}.

•

For any
s\geq 0
,
\mu,\mathrm{bo}^{-1}\in(0,1)
we denote
X^{s}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})=H^{s+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}({\mathbb{R}})\times H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
with norm

|(\zeta,u)|_{X^{s}_{\mu,\mathrm{bo}}}^{2}=|\zeta|_{H^{s+1}_{\mathrm{bo}}}^{2}+|u|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\sqrt{\mu}||\mathrm{D}|^{\frac{1}{2}}u|_{H^{s}}^{2}.

•

We say
f
is a Schwartz function
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
, if
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and satisfies for all
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup\limits_{x}|x^{j}\partial_{x}^{k}f|<\infty.

•

Let
a<b
be real numbers and consider the domain
\mathcal{S}=(a,b)\times{\mathbb{R}}
. Then the space
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{S})
is endowed with the seminorm

\|f\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{s}(\mathcal{S})}=\int_{a}^{b}|\nabla_{x,z}f(\cdot,z)|_{H^{s-1}}^{2}\>\mathrm{d}z.

•

If
A
and
B
are two operators, then we denote the commutator between them to be
[A,B]=AB-BA
.

•

Let
t_{0}>\frac{1}{2}
,
s\geq 0
, and
h_{\min}\in(0,1)
. Then for
C(\cdot)
being a positive non-decreasing function of its argument, we define the constants

M=C(\frac{1}{h_{\min}},|\zeta|_{H^{t_{0}+2}}),

and

M(s)=C(M,|\zeta|_{H^{s}}).

1.4.1.
Diffeomorphisms

In many instances it is convenient to “straighten”  the fluid domain. In particular, instead of working on the fluid domain
\Omega^{\pm}_{t}
we introduce the two strips:

\mathcal{S}^{+}=\{(x,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\>:\>-1<z<0\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{S}^{-}=\{(x,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\>:\>0<z\}.

The mapping between
\mathcal{S}^{\pm}
and
\Omega_{t}^{\pm}
will be given by the trivial diffeomorphisms defined below.

Definition 1.23
.

Let
t_{0}>\frac{1}{2}
and
\zeta\in H^{t_{0}+2}(\mathbb{R})
such that the non-cavitation assumptions (
1.16
) is satisfied. For the lower domain, we have that:

1.

We define the time-dependent diffeomorphism mapping the lower domain
\mathcal{S}^{+}
onto the water domain
\Omega_{t}^{+}
through

\Sigma^{+}:\>\begin{cases}\mathcal{S}^{+}\hskip 14.22636pt\longrightarrow&\Omega_{t}^{+}\\
(x,z)\>\>\mapsto&(x,z(1+{\varepsilon}\zeta)+{\varepsilon}\zeta).\end{cases}

2.

The Jacobi matrix
J_{\Sigma^{+}}
is given by

J_{\Sigma^{+}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\varepsilon(1+z)\partial_{x}\zeta&(1+\varepsilon\zeta)\end{pmatrix},

and is bounded on
\mathcal{S}^{+}
. Moreover the determinant is given by
1+{\varepsilon}\zeta
and is bounded below due to the non-cavitation condition (
1.16
).

3.

The matrix associated with the change of variable for the Laplace problem is given by

\displaystyle P(\Sigma^{+})=\begin{pmatrix}(1+{\varepsilon}\zeta)&-{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}(z+1)\partial_{x}\zeta\\
-{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}(z+1)\partial_{x}\zeta&\frac{1+{\varepsilon}^{2}\mu(z+1)^{2}|\partial_{x}\zeta|^{2}}{1+{\varepsilon}\zeta}\end{pmatrix},

and is uniformly coercive. In fact, one can verify that it satisfies for all
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
and any
(X,z)\in\mathcal{S}^{+}
that

(1.39)

\displaystyle P(\Sigma^{+})\theta\cdot\theta\geq\frac{1}{1+M}|\theta|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\|P^{+}(\Sigma^{+})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq M.

Moreover, we define
\partial_{n}^{P^{+}}=\mathbf{e}_{z}\cdot P(\Sigma^{+})\nabla^{\mu}_{x,z}
.

Similarly, for the upper domain, we have that:

1.

We define the time-dependent diffeomorphism mapping the upper-half plane
\mathcal{S}^{-}
onto the water domain
\Omega_{t}^{-}
through the transformation

\Sigma^{-}:\>\begin{cases}\mathcal{S}^{-}\hskip 14.22636pt\longrightarrow&\Omega_{t}^{-}\\
(x,z)\>\>\mapsto&(x,z+{\varepsilon}\zeta).\end{cases}

2.

The Jacobi matrix
J_{\Sigma^{-}}
is given by

J_{\Sigma^{-}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
{\varepsilon}\partial_{x}\zeta&1\end{pmatrix},

and is bounded on
\mathcal{S}^{-}
. Moreover, the determinant is given by
1
.

3.

The matrix associated with the change of variable for the Laplace problem is given by

\displaystyle P(\Sigma^{-})=\begin{pmatrix}1&-{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}\partial_{x}\zeta\\
-{\varepsilon}\sqrt{\mu}\partial_{x}\zeta&1+{\varepsilon}^{2}\mu(\partial_{x}\zeta)^{2}\end{pmatrix},

and is uniformly coercive. In fact, one can verify that it satisfies for all
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and any
(x,z)\in\mathcal{S}^{-}
that

(1.40)

\displaystyle P(\Sigma^{-})\theta\cdot\theta\geq\frac{1}{1+M}|\theta|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\|P^{-}(\Sigma^{-})\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq M.

Moreover, we define
\partial_{n}^{P^{-}}=\mathbf{e}_{z}\cdot P(\Sigma^{-})\nabla^{\mu}_{x,z}
.

2.
Properties of
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}

In this section, we aim to give a rigorous meaning to the operator
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}
introduced formally in equation (
1.12
) and study its properties. The main results in this section will now be stated.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
t_{0}\geq 1
,
s\in[0,t_{0}+1]
, and
\zeta\in H^{t_{0}+2}({\mathbb{R}})
be such that (
1.16
) is satisfied. Then the mapping

(2.1)

\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\>:\>\begin{cases}\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})&\rightarrow H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})\\
\psi&\mapsto\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\Big(1-\gamma(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\Big)^{-1}\psi,\end{cases}

is well-defined and satisfies the following properties:

1.

For any
0\leq s\leq t_{0}+1
,there holds,

(2.2)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\mu^{\frac{3}{4}}M|\psi|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}},

and

(2.3)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}M|\partial_{x}\psi|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}.

2.

For any
0\leq s\leq t_{0}+\frac{1}{2}
there holds,

(2.4)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\mu M|\psi|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+1}},

and

(2.5)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi|_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\mu^{\frac{3}{4}}M|\partial_{x}\psi|_{H^{s}}.

3.

The operator is uniformly coercive on on
\psi\in\dot{H}_{\mu}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
,

(2.6)

|\psi|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}\leq M\big(\psi,\frac{1}{\mu}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi\big)_{L^{2}}.

4.

The bilinear form is symmetric on
\dot{H}_{\mu}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})\times\dot{H}_{\mu}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
,

(2.7)

\big(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi,\psi\big)_{L^{2}}=\big(\psi,\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi\big)_{L^{2}}.

5.

For all
s\in[0,t_{0}+1]
and
f,g\in\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
there holds,

(2.8)

|\big(\Lambda^{s}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]f,\Lambda^{s}g\big)_{L^{2}}|\leq\mu M|f|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}|g|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}.

Remark 2.2
.

Here we take
t_{0}\geq 1
since we will apply the result at high regularity. If one would take
t_{0}>\frac{1}{2}
then we would need
s\in[\max\{0,1-t_{0}\},t_{0}+1]
to enforce Remark
A.9
, which specifies the needed regularity to define
\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}
.

Remark 2.3
.

For the undisturbed case, we can use formulas (
A.3
) and (
A.19
) to find that

(2.9)

\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[0]=\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|\frac{\mathrm{tanh}(\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)}{1+\gamma\mathrm{tanh}(\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|)}.

We will follow the same strategy as in
[
65
]
, where we study the operators involved in the expression for
\mathcal{G}_{\mu}
and prove that we can define them by
\psi
through the transition problem (
1.11
). We study each part individually in separate subsections. The main difference from the previous work is that we have to carefully track the dependence on the parameters for the current regime. Moreover, the functional setting of the upper fluid is fundamentally different from the one in the lower fluid domain.

2.1.
Properties of
(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{-})^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{+}
.

For the description of the operator
(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{-})^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{+}
we will follow the proof of Proposition
1
in
[
65
]
. The main difference is that we have an interaction of two operators that act on different scales, and this is seen in the estimates given below:

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
t_{0}\geq 1
,
s\in[0,t_{0}+1]
and
\zeta\in H^{t_{0}+2}({\mathbb{R}})
be such that (
1.16
) is satisfied. Then the mapping

(2.10)

(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]^{-})^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\>:\>\begin{cases}\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})&\rightarrow\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})\\
\psi^{+}&\mapsto(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}\end{cases}

is well-defined and satisfies

(2.11)

|(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}|_{\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}M|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}},

and

(2.12)

|\partial_{x}\big((\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}\big)|_{H^{s}}\leq M|\partial_{x}\psi^{+}|_{H^{s}}.

Remark 2.5
.

If we change the role of
\pm
the result is not true. Indeed, consider the case
{\varepsilon}\zeta=0
, then we have by direct computations that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{+}[0])^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{-}[0]=(\tanh(\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|))^{-1},

and we obtain the estimate

|(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{+}[0])^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{-}[0]\psi^{-}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}|\psi^{-}|_{H^{\frac{1}{2}}},

which is incompatible with having
\psi^{-}\in\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
.

Before we turn to the proof, we need a Lemma that will be used to justify some of the computations that will be made.

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose the provisions of Proposition
2.4
, and further that
\phi^{-}=\Phi^{-}\circ\Sigma^{-}\in\dot{H}^{s+1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
is a variational solution to

(2.13)

\begin{cases}\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\cdot P(\Sigma^{-})\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\phi^{-}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\qquad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{S}^{-}\\
\partial_{n}^{P^{-}}\phi^{-}=\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}\hskip 59.75095pt\text{on}\quad z=0.\end{cases}

Then there is a number
R>0
such that for
\mathcal{S}^{-}_{R}=\{(x,z)\in\mathcal{S}^{-}\>:\>z>R\}
and any
\alpha+\beta
integer
>0
there holds,

(2.14)

\partial_{z}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}\phi^{-}\in L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{-}_{R})\quad\text{and}\quad\lim\limits_{z\rightarrow\infty}\sup\limits_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big|\partial_{z}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}\phi^{-}(x,z)\big|=0.

Moreover, for any
\varphi\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
have the following trace inequalities:

(2.15)

|\varphi^{-}|_{z=0}|_{\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\mu^{-\frac{1}{4}}\|\Lambda^{s}\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{-})},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

and

(2.16)

|\varphi|_{z=0}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\Lambda^{s}\nabla^{\mu}_{x,z}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{-})}.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

Remark 2.7
.

For the proof of (
2.15
), we need to work on the upper-half plane to prove the estimate, and this is the technical reason why we do not study the reverse composition:
(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{+})^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{-}
.

Remark 2.8
.

The proof of estimate (
2.14
) is given in
[
4
]
for
\phi|_{z=0}\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
and we will briefly explain the changes for
\phi|_{z=0}\in\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
.

Assuming for a moment that Lemma
2.6
holds true, we can give the proof of Proposition
2.4
.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

We divide the proof into four main steps.

Step 1.

(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{-})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{+}
is well-defined on
\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
. It is sufficient to prove that there exists a unique variational solution
\phi^{-}\in\dot{H}^{s+1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
to the system (
2.13
) for any
\psi^{+}\in\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}({\mathbb{R}})
. Indeed, assuming there is such a solution, then by (
2.15
), we can define

\psi^{-}=\phi^{-}|_{z=0}\in\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}}),

so that for any
\psi^{+}\in\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}({\mathbb{R}})
there is a
\psi^{-}
where Proposition
A.13
implies

\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{-}=\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}.

To prove the claim we first let
s=0
, and use (
2.14
) to define the variational problem associated to (
2.13
) by

(2.17)

\displaystyle a(\phi^{-},\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}^{-}}P(\Sigma^{-})\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\phi^{-}\cdot\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\varphi\>\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=-\int_{\{z=0\}}(\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+})\varphi\>\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=L(\varphi),

for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{S}^{-}})\cap\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
. We will now verify the assumptions of Lax-Milgram’s Theorem to deduce a variational solution in
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
in two steps (extending the result to
\dot{H}^{s+1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
is classical).

The first step is to show that the application
\varphi\mapsto L(\varphi)
is continuous on
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
. To do so, we note by estimate (
A.6
) that

\displaystyle|L(\varphi)|

\displaystyle\leq|\big(\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+},\varphi|_{z=0}\big)_{L^{2}}|

\displaystyle\leq\mu|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}}|\varphi|_{z=0}|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}}.

Then use (
2.16
) to obtain the needed bound

(2.18)

|\varphi|_{z=0}|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}}\leq\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\|\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{-})}.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

Lastly, The bilinear form
a(\cdot,\cdot)
is continuous and coercive on
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
by using estimate (
1.40
). We may therefore conclude that there is a unique solution
\phi^{-}\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
.

Step 2.

Estimate (
2.11
) holds true.
Let
\psi^{-}=\phi^{-}|_{z=0}\in\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}
defined by the solution of (
2.13
). Then we can use estimate (
A.22
) to get that

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}|_{\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=|\psi^{-}|_{\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\mu^{-\frac{1}{4}}M\|\Lambda^{s}\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\phi^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{+})}.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

Thus, we need to verify

(2.19)

\|\Lambda^{s}\nabla^{\mu}\phi^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{-})}\leq\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}M|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}.

To this end, we apply
\Lambda^{s}
to (
2.13
) and study:

\begin{cases}\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\cdot P(\Sigma^{-})\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\Lambda^{s}\phi^{-}=-\nabla_{x,z}[P(\Sigma^{-}),\Lambda^{s}]\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\phi^{-}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{in}\quad\mathcal{S}^{-}\\
\partial_{n}^{P^{-}}\Lambda^{s}\phi^{-}=-\mathbf{e}_{z}\cdot[P(\Sigma^{-}),\Lambda^{s}]\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\phi^{-}+\Lambda^{s}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}\hskip 21.33955pt\text{on}\quad z=0.\end{cases}

We may therefore multiply the equation with
\Lambda^{s}\phi^{-}
and integrate by parts on a finite domain
\mathcal{S}^{-}_{R}=\{(x,z)\in\mathcal{S}^{-}\>:\>0<z<R\}
and use the Dominated Convergence Theorem with (
2.14
) to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}^{-}}P(\Sigma^{-})\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\Lambda^{s}\phi^{-}\cdot\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\Lambda^{s}\phi^{-}\>\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=-\int_{\{z=0\}}\Lambda^{s}(\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+})\Lambda^{s}\phi^{-}\>\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int_{\mathcal{S}^{-}}[P(\Sigma^{-}),\Lambda^{s}]\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\phi^{-}\cdot\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\Lambda^{s}\phi^{-}\>\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z.

Now, by the coercivity estimate (
1.40
), Cauchy-Schwarz, (
A.6
), (
2.16
), and the commutator estimate (
B.16
) we get that

(2.20)

\displaystyle\|\Lambda^{s}\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\phi^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{-})}\leq M\big(\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}}+\|\Lambda^{s-1}\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\phi^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{-})}\big).\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

Also, using the estimates in Step
1
, we obtain the base case

(2.21)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\phi^{-}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{-})}\leq\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}M|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}}.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

We may now conclude the proof of this step by continuous induction.

Step 3.
To prove estimate (
2.12
) we first observe that

\langle\xi\rangle^{s}|\xi|\lesssim\langle\xi\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}|\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\xi\rangle^{s+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{|\xi|}{(1+\sqrt{\mu}|\xi|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim\mu^{-\frac{1}{4}}\langle\xi\rangle^{s}|\xi|.

Then use Plancherel’s identity and (
2.11
) to find that

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}\big((\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\circ\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}\big)|_{H^{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim|(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\circ\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}|_{\mathring{H}^{(s+\frac{1}{2})+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\mu^{\frac{1}{4}}M|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{(s+\frac{1}{2})+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq M|\partial_{x}\psi^{+}|_{H^{s}}.

∎

To close this subsection, we give the proof of Lemma
2.6
.

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

We first give a proof of the trace inequalities on
C^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{S}^{-}})\cap\dot{H}^{1}(\mathcal{S}^{-})
.
For the proof of (
2.15
), we define a multiplier being a smooth cut-off function in frequency
\chi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,1]
such that
\chi(0)=1
,
\chi(\xi)=0
for
\xi>1
, and
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
. Then by the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus we obtain that

\displaystyle||\mathrm{D}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi|_{z=0}|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{0}^{\frac{2}{\sqrt{\mu}|\xi|}}\partial_{z}\big(\chi(z\sqrt{\mu}|\xi|)\big||\xi|^{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{\varphi}\big|^{2}\big)\>\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\xi\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\mu}|\chi^{\prime}|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big||\xi|\hat{\varphi}\big|^{2}\>\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\xi+|\chi|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big||\xi|\hat{\varphi}\big|\big|\partial_{z}\hat{\varphi}\big|\>\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\xi.

For the last term, we use Young’s inequality to find that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big||\xi|\hat{\varphi}\big|\big|\partial_{z}\hat{\varphi}\big|\>\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\xi\lesssim\sqrt{\mu}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big||\xi|\hat{\varphi}\big|^{2}\>\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\xi+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\partial_{z}\hat{\varphi}\big|^{2}\>\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\xi

Then to conclude, we use Plancherel’s identity to obtain (
2.15
) for
s=0
:

\displaystyle||\mathrm{D}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\varphi|_{z=0}|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\big|\sqrt{\mu}|\xi|\hat{\varphi}\big|^{2}+\big|\partial_{z}\hat{\varphi}\big|^{2}\Big)\>\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}\xi

\displaystyle\lesssim\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{-})}^{2}.

The general case is proved similarly by applying the same estimate to
\tilde{\varphi}=\Lambda^{s}\varphi
.

For the proof of (
2.16
) we simply use that
|\varphi|_{z=0}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\leq\mu^{-\frac{1}{4}}|\varphi|_{z=0}|_{\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}
and then apply (
2.15
) to conclude.

For the proof of (
2.14
), we note from Definition
1.23
that
\Phi^{-}=\phi^{-}\circ{\Sigma}^{-1}\in\dot{H}^{s+1}(\Omega^{-})
is harmonic, and by Sobolev embedding we have that
\zeta
is bounded above by some constant
R>0
. From these observations, we can use (
2.15
) to see that
\Phi^{-}|_{z=R}\in\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
. Moreover, we consider the harmonic extension on
\mathcal{S}^{-}_{R}
given by the Poisson formula:

\Phi^{-}_{R}(x,z)=\mathrm{e}^{-(z-R)\sqrt{\mu}|\mathrm{D}|}\Phi^{-}(x,R).

From this formula, we can verify (
2.14
) for
\Phi^{-}_{R}
by direct computations as in
[
4
]
. To conclude, we use that both functions are harmonic and agree on the line
z=R
.

∎

We should note that there are several results that follow from Proposition
2.4
, and that will be used throughout the paper. However, to ease the presentation, we postponed these results for the Appendix in Section
A.3
since the proofs are technical and not needed in this section.

2.2.
Properties of
(1-\gamma(\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{-})^{-1}\mathcal{G}_{\mu}^{+})^{-1}
.

Following the road map provided in
[
65
]
, we can recover the velocity potentials
\phi^{\pm}
from the knowledge of
\zeta
and a trace
\psi
defined though the transmission problem:

(2.22)

\begin{cases}\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu^{\pm}}\cdot P(\Sigma^{\pm})\nabla_{x,z}^{\mu^{\pm}}\phi^{\pm}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\qquad\text{in}\quad\mathcal{S}^{\pm}\\
\phi^{+}|_{z=0}-\gamma\phi^{-}|_{z=0}=\psi\\
\partial_{n}^{P^{-}}\phi^{-}|_{z=0}=\partial_{n}^{P^{+}}\phi^{+}|_{z=0},\quad\partial_{n}^{P^{+}}\phi^{+}|_{z=-1}=0,\end{cases}

where the solvability of this problem is ensured in the next result:

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
t_{0}\geq 1
,
s\in[0,t_{0}+1]
and
\zeta\in H^{t_{0}+2}({\mathbb{R}})
be such that (
1.16
) is satisfied. Then for all
\psi\in\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})
, there exist a unique solution
\phi^{\pm}\in\dot{H}^{s+1}(\mathcal{S}^{\pm})
to (
2.22
) and that satisfies

\|\Lambda^{s}\nabla^{\mu}_{x,z}\phi^{\pm}\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}^{+})}\leq\sqrt{\mu}M|\psi|_{\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}}.

The proof of this result is a consequence of the following Lemma:

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
t_{0}\geq 1
,
s\in[0,t_{0}+1]
. Then the mapping

(2.23)

\mathcal{J}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\>:\>\begin{cases}\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})&\rightarrow\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}})\\
\psi^{+}&\mapsto(1-\gamma(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])\psi^{+}\end{cases}

is one-to-one and onto. Moreover, it satisfies the estimate

(2.24)

|(\mathcal{J}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}\leq M|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}.

Remark 2.11
.

For any
\psi\in\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}({\mathbb{R}})
we can define
\psi^{+}
by

\psi^{+}=(\mathcal{J}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\psi\in\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}_{\mu}({\mathbb{R}}),

from which we define
\psi^{-}
by

\displaystyle\psi^{-}=(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}\in\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}({\mathbb{R}}).

Also, note that from these identities and (
2.11
) that there holds,

(2.25)

\displaystyle|\psi|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=|\mathcal{J}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\gamma|(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}+\gamma\mu^{-\frac{1}{4}}|(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}|_{\mathring{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq M|\psi^{+}|_{\dot{H}_{\mu}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}}.

Proof of Lemma
2.10
.

To prove estimate (
2.24
), we first consider the case
s=0
. Then we use the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]
, the construction
\psi^{-}=(\mathcal{G}^{-}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta])^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}
to get that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{J}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}\>\mathcal{G}^{+}_{\mu}[{\varepsilon}\zeta]\psi^{+}\>\mathrm{d}x
